Time-varying.\n    39→- Trial outcome. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   111→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   112→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   113→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   114→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   115→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   116→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   117→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   118→\n   119→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   120→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   121→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   122→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   123→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   124→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   125→\n   126→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   127→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   128→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   129→\n   130→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   131→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   132→\n   133→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   134→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   135→\n   136→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   137→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   138→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   139→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   140→\n   141→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   142→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   143→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   144→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   145→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   146→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   147→\n   148→## Decoder Reference\n   149→\n   150→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   151→- Optional arguments:\n   152→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   153→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   154→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   155→\n   156→### Purpose\n   157→\n   158→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   159→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   160→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   161→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   162→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   163→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   164→\n   165→### Consistency requirements\n   166→\n   167→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n   168→- Loading of data\n   169→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   170→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   171→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   172→\n   173→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. Time-varying.\n- Pupil diameter, discretized into five equal percentile bins.  Time-varying.\n- Trial outcome. description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n\n### Consistency requirements\n\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\n- Loading of data\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\n\n## Success Criteria\n\nA successful conversion should:\n1. All required files must be created:\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\n  - Decoder accuracy\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\n","numLines":189,"startLine":1,"totalLines":189}}}

The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n    80→\n    81→ii.\n    82→```python\n    83→valid_trials = trials_table[\n    84→    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    85→    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    86→    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n    87→]\n    88→...\n    89→if end_idx > T:\n    90→    end_idx = T\n    91→if end_idx <= start_idx:\n    92→    continue\n    93→...\n    94→if len(trials) < 2:\n    95→    continue\n    96→```\n    97→\n    98→iii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n    99→\n   100→## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n   101→\n   102→i. The only processing is combining neurons from multiple imaging planes within a session by vertically stacking their dF/F arrays. Each neuron is tagged with a plane label (`{area}_{depth}um`) for brain region tracking.\n   116→\n   117→ii.\n   118→```python\n   119→dff_list = []\n   120→plane_labels = []\n   121→for exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n   122→    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n   123→    area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n   124→    depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n   125→    plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\n   126→neural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n   127→```\n   128→\n   129→iii. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n   134→\n   135→ii. N/A\n   136→\n   137→iii. The Allen SDK pipeline already applies its own quality control (e.g., cell segmentation, neuropil correction). No further filtering was deemed necessary.\n   138→\n   139→## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n   140→\n   141→i. Each trial's neural data is aligned to the trial start (`start_time`). Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n   153→\n   154→## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n   155→\n   156→i. The neural data is not binned or resampled. It is kept at the native ophys frame rate (~11 Hz). What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n   167→\n   168→i. Image name is a time-varying variable derived from both `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` columns in the trials table, combined with `change_time` to determine when the image switches.\n   169→\n   170→ii.\n   171→```python\n   172→change_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\n   173→image_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\n   174→image_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\n   175→image_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n   176→```\n   177→\n   178→iii. Since the trial window now spans both pre- and post-change periods, image identity varies within a trial. Before `change_time`, the initial image is on screen; after, the change image is displayed. For catch trials (sham change), `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` are the same, so the image identity is constant throughout.\n   179→\n   180→## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n   181→\n   182→i. Image names are mapped to integer codes via a global mapping built from all unique image names across all sessions. The integer code varies within a trial (initial image code before change, change image code after).\n   183→\n   184→ii.\n   185→```python\n   186→all_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\n   187→image_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n   188→...\n   189→image_row = np.array(\n   190→    [image_to_code[name] for name in t['image_names']],\n   191→    dtype=np.int8)\n   192→```\n   193→\n   194→iii. A global mapping ensures consistent integer codes across sessions. Sorting the image names makes the mapping deterministic. The mapping is stored in `metadata['image_to_code']` so it can be recovered for interpretation.\n   195→\n   196→## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n   197→\n   198→i. Image name is computed per ophys frame within the trial window, using the same `idx` array as the neural data. It is 1 only during the first image flash and following grey period (750ms window starting at `change_time`), and only for **go trials** where an actual image change occurs. The 750ms window corresponds to one stimulus flash (250ms) plus the following grey inter-stimulus interval (500ms). This marks only the transient change event rather than the entire post-change period. Running speed is linearly interpolated from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values (from extrapolation) are mapped to bin 0.\n   253→\n   254→ii.\n   255→```python\n   256→f_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n   257→                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   258→running_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n   259→...\n   260→all_running = np.concatenate([\n   261→    np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n   262→    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n   263→])\n   264→run_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n   265→...\n   266→run_disc = apply_discretize(t['running'], run_edges)\n   267→```\n   268→\n   269→iii. Linear interpolation preserves the signal shape while resampling to the ophys timebase. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts across bins, which is important for balanced decoding. Bin edges are computed globally across all sessions to maintain consistent categories.\n   270→\n   271→## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n   272→\n   273→i. Running speed is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data. Based on the AllenSDK code, the clock used for different data stream are synced at the hardware level, so we can safely interpolate.\n   287→\n   288→## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Pupil diameter* derived from?\n   289→\n   290→i. Pupil diameter is derived from `dataset.eye_tracking`, using the `pupil_width` column. Blink frames (where `likely_blink` is True) are excluded before interpolation.\n   291→\n   292→ii.\n   293→```python\n   294→eye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\n   295→eye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\n   296→```\n   297→\n   298→iii. `pupil_width` was used as the measure of pupil diameter. Blink frames were removed prior to interpolation to avoid corrupting the signal with blink artifacts. The SDK's `likely_blink` flag provides a pre-computed blink detector.\n   299→\n   300→## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n   301→\n   302→i. Pupil diameter is linearly interpolated (after blink removal) from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. Same approach as running speed: linear interpolation to the ophys timebase, then global percentile-based discretization. Blink removal before interpolation prevents blink artifacts from propagating into neighboring timepoints.\n   321→\n   322→## 6-d. How is `output` *Pupil diameter* aligned with the neural data?\n   323→\n   324→i. Same approach as running speed — pupil diameter is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data.\n   325→\n   326→ii.\n   327→```python\n   328→eye = ref_ds.eye_tracking\n   329→eye_clean = eye[~eye['likely_blink']]\n   330→f_pupil = interp1d(eye_clean['timestamps'].values,\n   331→                    eye_clean['pupil_width'].values,\n   332→                    kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\n   333→pupil_diameter = f_pupil(ophys_ts)\n   334→...\n   335→'pupil': pupil_diameter[idx].astype(np.float32),\n   336→'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n   337→```\n   338→\n   339→iii. How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. Subjects (mice) and sessions are discovered from the Allen SDK's `VisualBehaviorOphysProjectCache`, filtered to the `VisualBehavior` project code. All data are pulled using the `get_ophys_experiment_table()` method. For each mouse, all ophys sessions are loaded via `get_behavior_ophys_experiment()`. Finding all data:\n```python\nbc = bpc.VisualBehaviorOphysProjectCache.from_s3_cache(cache_dir=cache_dir)\nexperiment_table = bc.get_ophys_experiment_table()\nvb_experiments = experiment_table[experiment_table.project_code == PROJECT_CODE]\nall_mouse_ids = sorted(vb_experiments.mouse_id.unique())\n```\n\nLoading a session's experiments:\n```python\nfor exp_id in session_experiments.index:\n    datasets[exp_id] = bc.get_behavior_ophys_experiment(exp_id)\n```\n\niii. Each experiment corresponds to one imaging plane in one session; sessions are reconsturcted by combining `experiments` are grouped by the same `ophys_session_id`.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects?\n\ni. Subjects correspond to unique `mouse_id` values in the experiment table.\n\nii.\n```python\nall_mouse_ids = sorted(vb_experiments.mouse_id.unique())\n```\n\niii. The `mouse_id` field is the SDK's unique identifier for each animal. The number of mice can be cross-checked against the project documentation.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each session corresponds to a unique `ophys_session_id` in the experiment table. A single session may contain multiple experiments (imaging planes), which are grouped together by matching `ophys_session_id`. Sessions for each mouse are sorted by `date_of_acquisition`.\n\nii.\n```python\nmouse_exps = vb_experiments[vb_experiments.mouse_id == mouse_id]\nmouse_sessions = mouse_exps.drop_duplicates(subset='ophys_session_id')[\n    ['ophys_session_id', 'session_type', 'date_of_acquisition']\n].sort_values(by='date_of_acquisition')\nsession_ids = mouse_sessions['ophys_session_id'].values\n...\nsess_exps = mouse_exps[mouse_exps.ophys_session_id == sid]\n```\n\niii. The `ophys_session_id` groups all imaging planes recorded simultaneously in one behavioral session. Sorting by acquisition date preserves the chronological order of sessions within each mouse.\n\n## 1-d. Are the data correctly split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are defined using the built-in `dataset.trials` table from the Allen SDK. Each trial corresponds to one stimulus change event (go or catch). The full trial window from `start_time` to `stop_time` is used, giving variable-length trials (typically ~80-90 frames / ~8s). Aborted trials, auto-rewarded trials, and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded.\n\nii.\n```python\ntrials_table = ref_ds.trials\n...\nvalid_trials = trials_table[\n    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n]\n\nfor _, row in valid_trials.iterrows():\n    start_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\n    end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\n    if end_idx > T:\n        end_idx = T\n    if end_idx <= start_idx:\n        continue\n    idx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n```\n\niii. The SDK's built-in trials table was used, since it provides pre-computed trial metadata (outcome, image identity, timing). The full trial window (`start_time` to `stop_time`) is used rather than a fixed window around `change_time`, so that the trial includes both pre-change stimulus flashes and the post-change response window. This enables time-varying output variables (image identity changes mid-trial). Trials are typically ~8s: ~4s pre-change (variable number of stimulus flashes) and ~4.2s post-change (fixed response window).\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Aborted trials (early lick before change), auto-rewarded trials (free reward), and trials without a valid `change_time` are excluded. Trials where `end_idx <= start_idx` (empty window) are skipped. If `stop_time` extends past the recording, the trial is clipped to the end. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded from the final output.\n\nii.\n```python\nvalid_trials = trials_table[\n    (~trials_table['aborted']) &\n    (~trials_table['auto_rewarded']) &\n    (trials_table['change_time'].notna())\n]\n...\nif end_idx > T:\n    end_idx = T\nif end_idx <= start_idx:\n    continue\n...\nif len(trials) < 2:\n    continue\n```\n\niii. Per instruction, we are ignoring aborted and auto-rewarded trials. Aborted trials were excluded because the mouse licked before the change occurred, so no change stimulus was presented. Auto-rewarded trials were excluded because the free reward biases the behavioral response. Requiring a valid `change_time` ensures there is a well-defined change point within the trial. The minimum 2-trial threshold prevents degenerate sessions from entering the dataset.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the final `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from the `dff_traces` (dF/F calcium fluorescence traces) from each experiment in the session, accessed via `dataset.dff_traces.dff`.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\nneural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n```\n\niii. dF/F is the standard measure of neural activity for two-photon calcium imaging. The Allen SDK provides it pre-computed with neuropil correction and baseline normalization already applied.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. The only processing is combining neurons from multiple imaging planes within a session by vertically stacking their dF/F arrays. Each neuron is tagged with a plane label (`{area}_{depth}um`) for brain region tracking.\n\nii.\n```python\ndff_list = []\nplane_labels = []\nfor exp_id, ds in datasets.items():\n    dff_list.append(np.vstack(ds.dff_traces.dff.values))\n    area = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'targeted_structure']\n    depth = session_experiments.loc[exp_id, 'imaging_depth']\n    plane_labels.extend([f'{area}_{depth}um'] * len(ds.dff_traces))\nneural_data = np.vstack(dff_list)  # (N_neurons, T)\n```\n\niii. The dF/F traces are already processed by the Allen SDK pipeline (motion correction, neuropil subtraction, dF/F normalization). No additional filtering or normalization was applied. Plane labels were constructed to preserve brain region information for downstream analysis.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. No additional quality filtering is applied to the neural data. All neurons present in the SDK's `dff_traces` are included.\n\nii. N/A\n\niii. The Allen SDK pipeline already applies its own quality control (e.g., cell segmentation, neuropil correction). No further filtering was deemed necessary.\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Each trial's neural data is aligned to the trial start (`start_time`). The ophys frames from `start_time` to `stop_time` are extracted, giving a variable-length window per trial.\n\nii.\n```python\nstart_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['start_time'])\nend_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts, row['stop_time'])\nidx = np.arange(start_idx, end_idx)\n...\n'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n```\n\niii. `np.searchsorted` finds the first ophys frame at or after each boundary time. Since the frame rate is ~11 Hz, the maximum alignment error is ~45 ms (half a frame). The full trial window is used so that time-varying output variables (image identity, image change) can capture the pre- and post-change periods.\n\n## 2-e. How is the `neural` data temporally binned/resampled?\n\ni. The neural data is not binned or resampled. It is kept at the native ophys frame rate (~11 Hz). The time bin size is computed from the median inter-frame interval of `ophys_timestamps`.\n\nii.\n```python\nfirst_ophys_ts = session_results[0][1]['ophys_ts']\ntime_bin_size_ms = float(np.median(np.diff(first_ophys_ts)) * 1000)\n```\n\niii. The ophys timestamps are already at a consistent frame rate determined by the microscope scanning. No resampling is needed since all data (neural, running, pupil) are aligned to the same ophys timebase.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image identity* derived from?\n\ni. Image name is a time-varying variable derived from both `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` columns in the trials table, combined with `change_time` to determine when the image switches.\n\nii.\n```python\nchange_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\nimage_names = np.empty(n_frames, dtype=object)\nimage_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\nimage_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n```\n\niii. Since the trial window now spans both pre- and post-change periods, image identity varies within a trial. Before `change_time`, the initial image is on screen; after, the change image is displayed. For catch trials (sham change), `initial_image_name` and `change_image_name` are the same, so the image identity is constant throughout.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Image identity*?\n\ni. Image names are mapped to integer codes via a global mapping built from all unique image names across all sessions. The integer code varies within a trial (initial image code before change, change image code after).\n\nii.\n```python\nall_image_names = sorted(all_image_names)\nimage_to_code = {name: i for i, name in enumerate(all_image_names)}\n...\nimage_row = np.array(\n    [image_to_code[name] for name in t['image_names']],\n    dtype=np.int8)\n```\n\niii. A global mapping ensures consistent integer codes across sessions. Sorting the image names makes the mapping deterministic. The mapping is stored in `metadata['image_to_code']` so it can be recovered for interpretation.\n\n## 3-c. How is `output` *Image identity* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Image name is computed per ophys frame within the trial window, using the same `idx` array as the neural data. The switch point is determined by `np.searchsorted` on `change_time`.\n\nii.\n```python\nchange_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'])\nimage_names[:change_idx] = row['initial_image_name']\nimage_names[change_idx:] = row['change_image_name']\n```\n\niii. The image identity at each frame is determined by whether that frame falls before or after `change_time`. This is aligned to the neural data because both use the same ophys frame indices.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Image change* derived from?\n\ni. Image change is a binary time-varying variable derived from `change_time` and the `go` column in the trials table. It is 1 only during the first image flash and following grey period (750ms window starting at `change_time`), and only for **go trials** where an actual image change occurs. For catch trials (sham change), `image_change` is 0 throughout.\n\nii.\n```python\nimage_change = np.zeros(n_frames, dtype=np.int8)\nif row['go']:\n    change_end_idx = np.searchsorted(ophys_ts[idx], row['change_time'] + 0.75)\n    image_change[change_idx:change_end_idx] = 1\n```\n\niii. The 750ms window corresponds to one stimulus flash (250ms) plus the following grey inter-stimulus interval (500ms). This marks only the transient change event rather than the entire post-change period. Catch trials are excluded because no actual image change occurs — the `go` column distinguishes real changes from sham changes.\n\n## 4-b. N/A\n\n## 4-d. How is `output` *Image change* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Same as image name — computed per ophys frame using the same `idx` array and `change_time` alignment.\n\nii. The `running_speed` attribute is the SDK's standard interface for locomotion data.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Running speed is linearly interpolated from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. NaN values (from extrapolation) are mapped to bin 0.\n\nii.\n```python\nf_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\nrunning_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n...\nall_running = np.concatenate([\n    np.concatenate([t['running'] for t in trials])\n    for _, _, trials in session_results if len(trials) > 0\n])\nrun_edges = discretize(all_running, N_LEVELS)\n...\nrun_disc = apply_discretize(t['running'], run_edges)\n```\n\niii. Linear interpolation preserves the signal shape while resampling to the ophys timebase. Percentile-based binning ensures roughly equal class counts across bins, which is important for balanced decoding. Bin edges are computed globally across all sessions to maintain consistent categories.\n\n## 5-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Running speed is interpolated to the ophys timebase before trial segmentation, so it shares the same time indices as the neural data. The same `idx` array is used to extract both.\n\nii.\n```python\nrun = ref_ds.running_speed\nf_run = interp1d(run['timestamps'].values, run['speed'].values,\n                 kind='linear', bounds_error=False, fill_value=np.nan)\nrunning_speed = f_run(ophys_ts)\n...\n'running': running_speed[idx].astype(np.float32),\n'neural': neural_data[:, idx].astype(np.float32),\n```\n\niii. By interpolating running speed onto `ophys_ts` upfront, alignment is guaranteed — both neural and running data are indexed by the same ophys frame indices. Based on the AllenSDK code, the clock used for different data stream are synced at the hardware level, so we can safely interpolate.\n\n## 6-a. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Pupil diameter*?\n\ni. Pupil diameter is linearly interpolated (after blink removal) from its native timestamps to the ophys timebase, then discretized into 5 percentile-based bins computed across all sessions. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Trial outcome* derived from?\n\ni. These four columns are the SDK's canonical trial outcome labels for the change detection task. They are mutually exclusive for non-aborted, non-auto-rewarded trials. The fallback `'other'` handles any edge cases, though in practice all valid trials should match one of the four outcomes.\n\n## 7-b. Only small metadata (`ophys_ts`, `plane_labels`) is retained for later use. The trial-level slices (already copied during `segment_trials`) are the only data kept.\n\nii.\n```python\nsession_meta = {\n    'ophys_ts': session_data['ophys_ts'],\n    'plane_labels': session_data['plane_labels'],\n}\ndel session_data\nsession_results.append((mouse_id, session_meta, trials))\n```\n\niii. The full-session `neural_data` array `(N_neurons, T)` is the largest object in memory. After `segment_trials` copies the per-trial slices, the full array is redundant. Briefly, in this go/no-go task, mice are presented with a continuous series of bri\\r\\nefly presented stimuli and they earn water rewards by correctly reporting when the identity of the image changes. Mice undergo standardized training in the beha\\r\\nvior facility prior to imaging, in which they first learn to detect orientation changes using static grating stimuli, then transition to flashed gratings, and s\\r\\nubsequently learn to detect changes in the identity of natural scene images. Each session included 8 images, for a total of 64 possible transitions. The images\\r\\nused in this study were a selected subset of the natural scenes stimulus set used in the Visual Coding Allen Brain Observatory.\\r\\n\\r\\n2-PHOTON CALCIUM IMAGING\\r\\nThis dataset includes single- and multi-plane 2-photon calcium imaging recordings from the visual cortex of transgenic mice expressing the calcium indicator GCa\\r\\nMP6 in populations of excitatory and inhibitory neurons. Microscopes include Scientifica VivoScope systems for single-plane imaging, or a modified Mesoscope sys\\r\\ntem (Multiscope) for multi-plane imaging.\\r\\n\\r\\nEXPERIMENTAL DESIGN\\r\\nOnce mice are well-trained in the task, they transition to performing the task while being imaged with a 2-photon microscope to enable simultaneous measurement\\r\\nof neural activity and behavior. During the imaging portion of the experiment, mice perform the task with the set of eight natural scene images they viewed duri\\r\\nng training, as well as a novel set of eight images that they had not seen before, in separate sessions. This allows evaluation of the impact of novelty on neur\\r\\nal coding for stimulus and behavioral information. Mice also undergo passive viewing sessions during the imaging stage, where the mice are given their daily wat\\r\\ner before the session (and are thus satiated) and view the task stimuli with the lick spout retracted so they are unable to earn water rewards. Lastly, during i\\r\\nmaging sessions only, stimuli were omitted with a 5% probability, disrupting the expected cadence of flashed image presentations. Stimulus changes and the stimu\\r\\nlus immediately preceding the change were never omitted.\\r\\n\\r\\nDATASET VARIANTS\\r\\nTo ensure that any observed effects of stimulus novelty are due to lack of experience with those images, rather than properties of the specific images that were\\r\\n used, we trained some mice with the opposite stimulus configuration such that the training set for these mice was the image set that was novel for a different\\r\\ngroup of mice (compare dataset variants VisualBehavior and VisualBehaviorTask1B in Figure 5). This control was performed using the single-plane 2-photon imaging\\r\\n system.\\r\\n\\r\\nDATA STRUCTURE AND TERMINOLOGY\\r\\nDue to our experimental design, involving repeated imaging of the same population of cells over multiple days, the use of both single- and multi-plane imaging s\\r\\nystems, and our strict quality control (QC) criteria that result in failures & retakes of specific session types, the complete 2-photon dataset for a given mous\\r\\ne can take many forms. The terminology described below is used to reference different subsets of the dataset, and these definitions apply to terms used in the A\\r\\nllenSDK, associated data manifest tables, and contents of NWB files.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe data collected in a single continuous recording is defined as a session. For single-plane imaging experiments, there is only one imaging plane (referred to\\r\\nas an experiment) per session. For multi-plane imaging experiments, there can be up to 8 imaging planes (8 experiments) per session. Due to our QC process, not\\r\\nall multi-plane imaging sessions have exactly 8 experiments, as some imaging planes did not meet our QC criteria. We aimed to track the activity of single neuro\\r\\nns across multiple session types (familiar and novel image sets, passive and active behavior sessions; described in the overview) by targeting the same populati\\r\\non of neurons over multiple recording sessions, with only one session recorded per day for a given mouse. The collection of imaging sessions for a given populat\\r\\nion of cells, belonging to a single imaging plane measured across days, is called a container. A container can include between 3 and 11 separate sessions for a\\r\\ngiven imaging plane. Accordingly, the session types available for a given container (corresponding to a specific imaging plane) can vary. Mice imaged with the m\\r\\nulti-plane 2-photon microscope can have multiple containers, one for each of the 8 imaging planes recorded by the Multiscope (after data quality review, some of\\r\\n the containers may fail and be removed from the dataset). Thus, each mouse can have one or more containers, each representing a unique imaging plane (experime\\n[... output limited to 10000 bytes; 71 interior bytes omitted ...]\\nlit\\r\\ntle adjustment, such as different mouse cell lines, fluorescent proteins (e.g., GCaMP6f or GCaMP6s), and FOV locations of visual areas and depths. The process b\\r\\negins with the full image sequence as input to apply both the spatial as well as temporal information to isolate an individual active cell of interest without d\\r\\nata reduction, such as by PCA, and does not make assumptions about the number of independent components existing in the active cell movie. Also, in contrast to\\r\\nother methods, this approach separates the individual steps, including identifying and isolating each cellular object, computing confidence of each identified o\\r\\nbject (by object classification) and the step of resolving objects overlapping in x-y space (which lead to cross talk in traces), so that each can be improved u\\r\\npon if necessary.\\r\\n\\r\\nCROSSTALK REMOVAL IN MULTISCOPE DATA\\r\\nCrosstalk between focal planes is a fundamental limitation in multiplexed microscopy systems, such as the Multiscope. Details about system hardware and methods\\r\\nused to optimize crosstalk in the data are described above and can be found in Lecoq et al., 2020. Crosstalk removal was performed on fluorescence traces using\\r\\nan ICA-based approach (implementation scikit-learn.FastICA), where independent components are estimated by minimizing Gaussianity of the data (Hyv\\u00e4rinen, 1999).\\r\\n The assumption is that the two planes are a mixed observation of two clean sources and are mixed linearly using a mixing matrix. We assume a mixing matrix of t\\r\\nhe form [[1-a, a], [b, 1-b]], where a and b are in [0,1), and probably around 0.15. After FastICA, we transform the resulting mixing matrix to be of this form t\\r\\no recover the proper scaling of the mixed signals. Prior to FastICA, data undergoes whitening; we do not use the built-in whitening of the FastICA module (as it\\r\\n appears to contain a bug that affects scaling of the outputs).\\r\\n\\r\\nROI FILTERING\\r\\nNot all ROIs generated by segmentation are complete individual cell bodies. To exclude ROIs that are not actually cell bodies from further analysis, the ROIs ar\\r\\ne labeled with a multi-label classifier where any ROIs that end up labeled are not considered cell bodies. The set of reasons to exclude an ROI are: the ROI is\\r\\na union of two or more cells; the ROI is a duplicate of another; the ROI is close to the edge of the FOV and is impacted by motion such that parts of the ROI ar\\r\\ne missing from the video; the ROI is likely an apical dendrite and not a cell body; or that the ROI is too small, too narrow, or too dim to confidently be consi\\r\\ndered a cell body.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe initial ROI filtering was generated by a set of heuristics based on depth, shape, area, intensity, signal-to-noise, and the ratio of mean to max intensity o\\r\\nf the max projection. The initial filtering was used to generate a set of training labels, on which a multi-label classifier was trained. The multi-label classi\\r\\nfier is implemented using a linear Support Vector Classifier trainer for each label (binary relevance) using metrics generated in segmentation combined with dep\\r\\nth, driver, reporter, and targeted structure as features. The final ROI filtering workflow is to 1) label ROIs that fall within the motion cutoff regions at the\\r\\n border, 2) label ROIs using the binary relevance classifier, 3) label significantly overlapping ROIs as duplicates, and 4) label ROIs that significantly overla\\r\\np two or more ROIs as unions.\\r\\n\\r\\nDEMIXING TRACES FROM OVERLAPPING ROIS\\r\\nThe simplest way to extract fluorescence traces, given a set of ROI masks, is to average the fluorescence within each ROI. If two ROIs overlap, this procedure w\\r\\nill artificially correlate their traces. Therefore, a model is used where every ROI has a trace which is distributed across its ROI in some spatially heterogene\\r\\nous, time-dependent fashion:\\r\\nF(it) = \\u2211_k W(kit) T(kt)\\r\\nwhere W is a tensor containing time-dependent weighted masks: W(kit) measures how much of neuron k's fluorescence is contained in pixel i at time t. T(kt) is th\\r\\ne fluorescence trace of neuron k at time t - this is the desired value to estimate. F(it) is the recorded fluorescence in pixel i at time t.\\r\\n\\r\\nImportantly, this model applies to all ROIs, including those too small to be a neuron or otherwise filtered out. Duplicate ROIs (defined as two ROIs with >70% o\\r\\nverlap) and ROIs that are the union of two other ROIs (any ROI where the union of any other two ROIs accounts for 70% of its area) are filtered out before demix\\r\\ning, and the remaining filtering criteria are applied after demixing. Projecting the movie (F) onto the binary masks (A) reduces the dimensionality of the probl\\r\\nem from 512x512 pixels to the number of ROIs:\\r\\n\\u2211_i A(ki) F(it) = \\u2211_{k,i} A(ki) W(kit) T(kt)\\r\\nwhere A(ki) is one if pixel i is in ROI k and zero otherwise\\u2013these are the ROI masks from segmentation, after filtering out duplicate and union ROIs. Briefly, in this go/no-go task, mice are presented with a continuous series of bri\\r\\nefly presented stimuli and they earn water rewards by correctly reporting when the identity of the image changes. Mice undergo standardized training in the beha\\r\\nvior facility prior to imaging, in which they first learn to detect orientation changes using static grating stimuli, then transition to flashed gratings, and s\\r\\nubsequently learn to detect changes in the identity of natural scene images. Each session included 8 images, for a total of 64 possible transitions. The images\\r\\nused in this study were a selected subset of the natural scenes stimulus set used in the Visual Coding Allen Brain Observatory.\\r\\n\\r\\n2-PHOTON CALCIUM IMAGING\\r\\nThis dataset includes single- and multi-plane 2-photon calcium imaging recordings from the visual cortex of transgenic mice expressing the calcium indicator GCa\\r\\nMP6 in populations of excitatory and inhibitory neurons. Microscopes include Scientifica VivoScope systems for single-plane imaging, or a modified Mesoscope sys\\r\\ntem (Multiscope) for multi-plane imaging.\\r\\n\\r\\nEXPERIMENTAL DESIGN\\r\\nOnce mice are well-trained in the task, they transition to performing the task while being imaged with a 2-photon microscope to enable simultaneous measurement\\r\\nof neural activity and behavior. During the imaging portion of the experiment, mice perform the task with the set of eight natural scene images they viewed duri\\r\\nng training, as well as a novel set of eight images that they had not seen before, in separate sessions. This allows evaluation of the impact of novelty on neur\\r\\nal coding for stimulus and behavioral information. Mice also undergo passive viewing sessions during the imaging stage, where the mice are given their daily wat\\r\\ner before the session (and are thus satiated) and view the task stimuli with the lick spout retracted so they are unable to earn water rewards. Lastly, during i\\r\\nmaging sessions only, stimuli were omitted with a 5% probability, disrupting the expected cadence of flashed image presentations. Stimulus changes and the stimu\\r\\nlus immediately preceding the change were never omitted.\\r\\n\\r\\nDATASET VARIANTS\\r\\nTo ensure that any observed effects of stimulus novelty are due to lack of experience with those images, rather than properties of the specific images that were\\r\\n used, we trained some mice with the opposite stimulus configuration such that the training set for these mice was the image set that was novel for a different\\r\\ngroup of mice (compare dataset variants VisualBehavior and VisualBehaviorTask1B in Figure 5). This control was performed using the single-plane 2-photon imaging\\r\\n system.\\r\\n\\r\\nDATA STRUCTURE AND TERMINOLOGY\\r\\nDue to our experimental design, involving repeated imaging of the same population of cells over multiple days, the use of both single- and multi-plane imaging s\\r\\nystems, and our strict quality control (QC) criteria that result in failures & retakes of specific session types, the complete 2-photon dataset for a given mous\\r\\ne can take many forms. The terminology described below is used to reference different subsets of the dataset, and these definitions apply to terms used in the A\\r\\nllenSDK, associated data manifest tables, and contents of NWB files.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe data collected in a single continuous recording is defined as a session. For single-plane imaging experiments, there is only one imaging plane (referred to\\r\\nas an experiment) per session. For multi-plane imaging experiments, there can be up to 8 imaging planes (8 experiments) per session. Due to our QC process, not\\r\\nall multi-plane imaging sessions have exactly 8 experiments, as some imaging planes did not meet our QC criteria. We aimed to track the activity of single neuro\\r\\nns across multiple session types (familiar and novel image sets, passive and active behavior sessions; described in the overview) by targeting the same populati\\r\\non of neurons over multiple recording sessions, with only one session recorded per day for a given mouse. The collection of imaging sessions for a given populat\\r\\nion of cells, belonging to a single imaging plane measured across days, is called a container. A container can include between 3 and 11 separate sessions for a\\r\\ngiven imaging plane. Accordingly, the session types available for a given container (corresponding to a specific imaging plane) can vary. Mice imaged with the m\\r\\nulti-plane 2-photon microscope can have multiple containers, one for each of the 8 imaging planes recorded by the Multiscope (after data quality review, some of\\r\\n the containers may fail and be removed from the dataset). Thus, each mouse can have one or more containers, each representing a unique imaging plane (experime\\n[... output limited to 10000 bytes; 71 interior bytes omitted ...]\\nlit\\r\\ntle adjustment, such as different mouse cell lines, fluorescent proteins (e.g., GCaMP6f or GCaMP6s), and FOV locations of visual areas and depths. The process b\\r\\negins with the full image sequence as input to apply both the spatial as well as temporal information to isolate an individual active cell of interest without d\\r\\nata reduction, such as by PCA, and does not make assumptions about the number of independent components existing in the active cell movie. Also, in contrast to\\r\\nother methods, this approach separates the individual steps, including identifying and isolating each cellular object, computing confidence of each identified o\\r\\nbject (by object classification) and the step of resolving objects overlapping in x-y space (which lead to cross talk in traces), so that each can be improved u\\r\\npon if necessary.\\r\\n\\r\\nCROSSTALK REMOVAL IN MULTISCOPE DATA\\r\\nCrosstalk between focal planes is a fundamental limitation in multiplexed microscopy systems, such as the Multiscope. Details about system hardware and methods\\r\\nused to optimize crosstalk in the data are described above and can be found in Lecoq et al., 2020. Crosstalk removal was performed on fluorescence traces using\\r\\nan ICA-based approach (implementation scikit-learn.FastICA), where independent components are estimated by minimizing Gaussianity of the data (Hyv\\u00e4rinen, 1999).\\r\\n The assumption is that the two planes are a mixed observation of two clean sources and are mixed linearly using a mixing matrix. We assume a mixing matrix of t\\r\\nhe form [[1-a, a], [b, 1-b]], where a and b are in [0,1), and probably around 0.15. After FastICA, we transform the resulting mixing matrix to be of this form t\\r\\no recover the proper scaling of the mixed signals. Prior to FastICA, data undergoes whitening; we do not use the built-in whitening of the FastICA module (as it\\r\\n appears to contain a bug that affects scaling of the outputs).\\r\\n\\r\\nROI FILTERING\\r\\nNot all ROIs generated by segmentation are complete individual cell bodies. To exclude ROIs that are not actually cell bodies from further analysis, the ROIs ar\\r\\ne labeled with a multi-label classifier where any ROIs that end up labeled are not considered cell bodies. The set of reasons to exclude an ROI are: the ROI is\\r\\na union of two or more cells; the ROI is a duplicate of another; the ROI is close to the edge of the FOV and is impacted by motion such that parts of the ROI ar\\r\\ne missing from the video; the ROI is likely an apical dendrite and not a cell body; or that the ROI is too small, too narrow, or too dim to confidently be consi\\r\\ndered a cell body.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe initial ROI filtering was generated by a set of heuristics based on depth, shape, area, intensity, signal-to-noise, and the ratio of mean to max intensity o\\r\\nf the max projection. The initial filtering was used to generate a set of training labels, on which a multi-label classifier was trained. The multi-label classi\\r\\nfier is implemented using a linear Support Vector Classifier trainer for each label (binary relevance) using metrics generated in segmentation combined with dep\\r\\nth, driver, reporter, and targeted structure as features. The final ROI filtering workflow is to 1) label ROIs that fall within the motion cutoff regions at the\\r\\n border, 2) label ROIs using the binary relevance classifier, 3) label significantly overlapping ROIs as duplicates, and 4) label ROIs that significantly overla\\r\\np two or more ROIs as unions.\\r\\n\\r\\nDEMIXING TRACES FROM OVERLAPPING ROIS\\r\\nThe simplest way to extract fluorescence traces, given a set of ROI masks, is to average the fluorescence within each ROI. If two ROIs overlap, this procedure w\\r\\nill artificially correlate their traces. Therefore, a model is used where every ROI has a trace which is distributed across its ROI in some spatially heterogene\\r\\nous, time-dependent fashion:\\r\\nF(it) = \\u2211_k W(kit) T(kt)\\r\\nwhere W is a tensor containing time-dependent weighted masks: W(kit) measures how much of neuron k's fluorescence is contained in pixel i at time t. T(kt) is th\\r\\ne fluorescence trace of neuron k at time t - this is the desired value to estimate. F(it) is the recorded fluorescence in pixel i at time t.\\r\\n\\r\\nImportantly, this model applies to all ROIs, including those too small to be a neuron or otherwise filtered out. Duplicate ROIs (defined as two ROIs with >70% o\\r\\nverlap) and ROIs that are the union of two other ROIs (any ROI where the union of any other two ROIs accounts for 70% of its area) are filtered out before demix\\r\\ning, and the remaining filtering criteria are applied after demixing. Projecting the movie (F) onto the binary masks (A) reduces the dimensionality of the probl\\r\\nem from 512x512 pixels to the number of ROIs:\\r\\n\\u2211_i A(ki) F(it) = \\u2211_{k,i} A(ki) W(kit) T(kt)\\r\\nwhere A(ki) is one if pixel i is in ROI k and zero otherwise\\u2013these are the ROI masks from segmentation, after filtering out duplicate and union ROIs. At a parti\\r\\ncular time point t, this yields the simple linear regression:\\r\\nroot@9ce7abf424aa:/app#\\r\\n\"\n          }\n        ]\n      },\n      \"metrics\": {\n        \"prompt_tokens\": 12136,\n        \"completion_tokens\": 244,\n        \"cached_tokens\": 9335,\n        \"cost_usd\": 0.03109837500000001\n      }\n    },\n    {\n      \"step_id\": 6,\n      \"timestamp\": \"2026-09-20T19:52:15.690958+00:00\",\n      \"source\": \"agent\",\n      \"model_name\": \"claude-opus-5\",\n      \"message\": \"Analysis: Reading methods.txt in chunks.\\nPlan: Print lines 40-130 etc. Actually the file is 211 lines with very long lines. description of output tasks\\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\\n        ...\\n    }\\n}\\n```\\n\\n### Key Format Requirements\\n\\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \\n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\\n\\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \\n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\\n\\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\\n\\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \\n\\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\\n\\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \\n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \\n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\\n5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\\n\\n### Consistency requirements\\n\\nYour processing and formatting must **match the reference papers and code** with respect to:\\n- Loading of data\\n- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\\n- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\\n- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\\n\\nDiscrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder. During testing, a reference copy of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code.\\n\\n## Success Criteria\\n\\nA successful conversion should:\\n1. All required files must be created:\\n  - `/app/convert_data.py`\\n  - `/app/converted_data.pkl`\\n2. Match the target data structure exactly\\n3. Have consistent dimensions across trials/sessions\\n4. Include all relevant data from the source\\n5. Match information provided in the reference texts, code, and these instructions\\n6. Outputs must match expert-written conversion code in:\\n  - Dataset size and distribution statistics\\n  - Decoder accuracy\\n7. Each decision about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. Decisions do not need to match the expert's: a different decision that is equally well justified is acceptable, while poorly justified decisions will be flagged as concerning or incorrect. Your code must implement the decisions as you describe them.\\n\\n\\nCurrent terminal state:\\nCurrent Terminal Screen:\\nroot@9ce7abf424aa:/app#\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\n\"\n    18→    },\n    19→    {\n    20→      \"step_id\": 2,\n    21→      \"timestamp\": \"2026-09-20T19:51:53.395625+00:00\",\n    22→      \"source\": \"agent\",\n    23→      \"model_name\": \"claude-opus-5\",\n    24→      \"message\": \"Analysis: Starting fresh. Briefly, in this go/no-go task, mice are presented with a continuous series of bri\\r\\nefly presented stimuli and they earn water rewards by correctly reporting when the identity of the image changes. Mice undergo standardized training in the beha\\r\\nvior facility prior to imaging, in which they first learn to detect orientation changes using static grating stimuli, then transition to flashed gratings, and s\\r\\nubsequently learn to detect changes in the identity of natural scene images. Each session included 8 images, for a total of 64 possible transitions. The images\\r\\nused in this study were a selected subset of the natural scenes stimulus set used in the Visual Coding Allen Brain Observatory.\\r\\n\\r\\n2-PHOTON CALCIUM IMAGING\\r\\nThis dataset includes single- and multi-plane 2-photon calcium imaging recordings from the visual cortex of transgenic mice expressing the calcium indicator GCa\\r\\nMP6 in populations of excitatory and inhibitory neurons. Microscopes include Scientifica VivoScope systems for single-plane imaging, or a modified Mesoscope sys\\r\\ntem (Multiscope) for multi-plane imaging.\\r\\n\\r\\nEXPERIMENTAL DESIGN\\r\\nOnce mice are well-trained in the task, they transition to performing the task while being imaged with a 2-photon microscope to enable simultaneous measurement\\r\\nof neural activity and behavior. During the imaging portion of the experiment, mice perform the task with the set of eight natural scene images they viewed duri\\r\\nng training, as well as a novel set of eight images that they had not seen before, in separate sessions. This allows evaluation of the impact of novelty on neur\\r\\nal coding for stimulus and behavioral information. Mice also undergo passive viewing sessions during the imaging stage, where the mice are given their daily wat\\r\\ner before the session (and are thus satiated) and view the task stimuli with the lick spout retracted so they are unable to earn water rewards. Lastly, during i\\r\\nmaging sessions only, stimuli were omitted with a 5% probability, disrupting the expected cadence of flashed image presentations. Stimulus changes and the stimu\\r\\nlus immediately preceding the change were never omitted.\\r\\n\\r\\nDATASET VARIANTS\\r\\nTo ensure that any observed effects of stimulus novelty are due to lack of experience with those images, rather than properties of the specific images that were\\r\\n used, we trained some mice with the opposite stimulus configuration such that the training set for these mice was the image set that was novel for a different\\r\\ngroup of mice (compare dataset variants VisualBehavior and VisualBehaviorTask1B in Figure 5). This control was performed using the single-plane 2-photon imaging\\r\\n system.\\r\\n\\r\\nDATA STRUCTURE AND TERMINOLOGY\\r\\nDue to our experimental design, involving repeated imaging of the same population of cells over multiple days, the use of both single- and multi-plane imaging s\\r\\nystems, and our strict quality control (QC) criteria that result in failures & retakes of specific session types, the complete 2-photon dataset for a given mous\\r\\ne can take many forms. The terminology described below is used to reference different subsets of the dataset, and these definitions apply to terms used in the A\\r\\nllenSDK, associated data manifest tables, and contents of NWB files.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe data collected in a single continuous recording is defined as a session. For single-plane imaging experiments, there is only one imaging plane (referred to\\r\\nas an experiment) per session. For multi-plane imaging experiments, there can be up to 8 imaging planes (8 experiments) per session. Due to our QC process, not\\r\\nall multi-plane imaging sessions have exactly 8 experiments, as some imaging planes did not meet our QC criteria. We aimed to track the activity of single neuro\\r\\nns across multiple session types (familiar and novel image sets, passive and active behavior sessions; described in the overview) by targeting the same populati\\r\\non of neurons over multiple recording sessions, with only one session recorded per day for a given mouse. The collection of imaging sessions for a given populat\\r\\nion of cells, belonging to a single imaging plane measured across days, is called a container. A container can include between 3 and 11 separate sessions for a\\r\\ngiven imaging plane. Accordingly, the session types available for a given container (corresponding to a specific imaging plane) can vary. Mice imaged with the m\\r\\nulti-plane 2-photon microscope can have multiple containers, one for each of the 8 imaging planes recorded by the Multiscope (after data quality review, some of\\r\\n the containers may fail and be removed from the dataset). Thus, each mouse can have one or more containers, each representing a unique imaging plane (experime\\n[... output limited to 10000 bytes; 71 interior bytes omitted ...]\\nlit\\r\\ntle adjustment, such as different mouse cell lines, fluorescent proteins (e.g., GCaMP6f or GCaMP6s), and FOV locations of visual areas and depths. The process b\\r\\negins with the full image sequence as input to apply both the spatial as well as temporal information to isolate an individual active cell of interest without d\\r\\nata reduction, such as by PCA, and does not make assumptions about the number of independent components existing in the active cell movie. Also, in contrast to\\r\\nother methods, this approach separates the individual steps, including identifying and isolating each cellular object, computing confidence of each identified o\\r\\nbject (by object classification) and the step of resolving objects overlapping in x-y space (which lead to cross talk in traces), so that each can be improved u\\r\\npon if necessary.\\r\\n\\r\\nCROSSTALK REMOVAL IN MULTISCOPE DATA\\r\\nCrosstalk between focal planes is a fundamental limitation in multiplexed microscopy systems, such as the Multiscope. Details about system hardware and methods\\r\\nused to optimize crosstalk in the data are described above and can be found in Lecoq et al., 2020. Crosstalk removal was performed on fluorescence traces using\\r\\nan ICA-based approach (implementation scikit-learn.FastICA), where independent components are estimated by minimizing Gaussianity of the data (Hyv\\u00e4rinen, 1999).\\r\\n The assumption is that the two planes are a mixed observation of two clean sources and are mixed linearly using a mixing matrix. We assume a mixing matrix of t\\r\\nhe form [[1-a, a], [b, 1-b]], where a and b are in [0,1), and probably around 0.15. After FastICA, we transform the resulting mixing matrix to be of this form t\\r\\no recover the proper scaling of the mixed signals. Prior to FastICA, data undergoes whitening; we do not use the built-in whitening of the FastICA module (as it\\r\\n appears to contain a bug that affects scaling of the outputs).\\r\\n\\r\\nROI FILTERING\\r\\nNot all ROIs generated by segmentation are complete individual cell bodies. To exclude ROIs that are not actually cell bodies from further analysis, the ROIs ar\\r\\ne labeled with a multi-label classifier where any ROIs that end up labeled are not considered cell bodies. The set of reasons to exclude an ROI are: the ROI is\\r\\na union of two or more cells; the ROI is a duplicate of another; the ROI is close to the edge of the FOV and is impacted by motion such that parts of the ROI ar\\r\\ne missing from the video; the ROI is likely an apical dendrite and not a cell body; or that the ROI is too small, too narrow, or too dim to confidently be consi\\r\\ndered a cell body.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe initial ROI filtering was generated by a set of heuristics based on depth, shape, area, intensity, signal-to-noise, and the ratio of mean to max intensity o\\r\\nf the max projection. The initial filtering was used to generate a set of training labels, on which a multi-label classifier was trained. The multi-label classi\\r\\nfier is implemented using a linear Support Vector Classifier trainer for each label (binary relevance) using metrics generated in segmentation combined with dep\\r\\nth, driver, reporter, and targeted structure as features. The final ROI filtering workflow is to 1) label ROIs that fall within the motion cutoff regions at the\\r\\n border, 2) label ROIs using the binary relevance classifier, 3) label significantly overlapping ROIs as duplicates, and 4) label ROIs that significantly overla\\r\\np two or more ROIs as unions.\\r\\n\\r\\nDEMIXING TRACES FROM OVERLAPPING ROIS\\r\\nThe simplest way to extract fluorescence traces, given a set of ROI masks, is to average the fluorescence within each ROI. If two ROIs overlap, this procedure w\\r\\nill artificially correlate their traces. Therefore, a model is used where every ROI has a trace which is distributed across its ROI in some spatially heterogene\\r\\nous, time-dependent fashion:\\r\\nF(it) = \\u2211_k W(kit) T(kt)\\r\\nwhere W is a tensor containing time-dependent weighted masks: W(kit) measures how much of neuron k's fluorescence is contained in pixel i at time t. T(kt) is th\\r\\ne fluorescence trace of neuron k at time t - this is the desired value to estimate. F(it) is the recorded fluorescence in pixel i at time t.\\r\\n\\r\\nImportantly, this model applies to all ROIs, including those too small to be a neuron or otherwise filtered out. Duplicate ROIs (defined as two ROIs with >70% o\\r\\nverlap) and ROIs that are the union of two other ROIs (any ROI where the union of any other two ROIs accounts for 70% of its area) are filtered out before demix\\r\\ning, and the remaining filtering criteria are applied after demixing. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01Wnx6TKybsyicNnzBvuXnC2","type":"tool_result","content":[{"type":"text","text":"I now have enough information to write a comprehensive summary. Here it is:\n\n---\n\n## Summary of Key Decisions in the Agent's `convert_data.py`\n\n### 1. Neural Signal: filtered_events (not dff_traces, not raw events)\n\nThe agent initially noted from the paper: \"For all analysis of neural data we used the detected calcium events.\" It first built a pilot with raw `events` (the sparse detected calcium events, ~0.3% nonzero frames), then compared against `filtered_events` (the same detected events convolved with a half-normal kernel). **filtered_events clearly won** on decoder accuracy (image identity 0.263 vs 0.202; change 0.636 vs 0.605; running 0.243 vs 0.220). The reasoning was that raw events are too sparse for per-timepoint decoding, while filtered_events preserves event timing but gives a continuous, non-sparse activity estimate more suitable for the decoder. `dff_traces` was never seriously considered -- the agent followed the paper's explicit guidance to use detected events rather than raw fluorescence.\n\nAdditionally, the agent applied **per-neuron normalization** (dividing each neuron by its within-session standard deviation), which further improved accuracy (image identity 0.284 vs 0.263). The justification was that it prevents high-variance cells from dominating the per-session PCA/projection used by the decoder.\n\n### 2. Image Identity: from stimulus_presentations table\n\nThe agent used `exp.stimulus_presentations` to construct the time-varying image identity signal. It filtered for active, non-omitted flashes (`sp.active == True` and `sp.omitted == False`), extracted `start_time`/`end_time` and `image_name`, and mapped each flash onto ophys frames using `np.searchsorted`. Gray screen / omitted periods were coded as 0; the 8 natural scene images were coded as 1-8. This is a time-varying categorical variable with 9 values (gray + 8 images).\n\n### 3. Image Change: from stimulus_presentations `is_change` flag\n\nThe agent used the `is_change` column from stimulus_presentations. For each flash marked as a change, the corresponding ophys frames (from flash start to flash end) were set to 1; all other frames were 0. This makes it a binary time-varying variable.\n\n### 4. Running Speed and Pupil Diameter\n\n- **Running speed**: Interpolated from `exp.running_speed` (sampled at ~60 Hz) onto the ophys frame timestamps using `np.interp`.\n- **Pupil diameter**: Derived from `exp.eye_tracking.pupil_area` by computing diameter as `2 * sqrt(area / pi)`. Blink frames (flagged by `likely_blink`) were set to NaN, then gaps were interpolated. Frames whose nearest valid pupil sample was more than 0.5 seconds away were flagged as invalid.\n\n### 5. Trial Filtering\n\n- Included only **go** and **catch** trials (from `exp.trials` where `tr.go | tr.catch`).\n- **Excluded** aborted and auto-rewarded trials, as explicitly required by the task.\n- Excluded trials shorter than 2 ophys frames.\n- Excluded trials with zero valid pupil measurements.\n- Excluded trials where the outcome was ambiguous (required exactly one of hit/miss/false_alarm/correct_reject to be True).\n- Final result: 21,624 trials kept out of ~21,750 go+catch trials (99.4% retention).\n\n### 6. Session Filtering: Active behavior, Familiar images, Single-plane (VisualBehavior project)\n\n- **Active vs passive**: Only active behavior sessions. Passive sessions lack trials and behavioral reports.\n- **Familiar vs novel**: Only familiar image sessions (experience_level == 'Familiar'), following the paper's restriction to familiar stimuli.\n- **Project code**: Only `VisualBehavior` (single-plane, ~31 Hz). The Multiscope sessions were excluded because they run at ~11 Hz, creating incompatible time bin sizes. Only 4 Multiscope sessions from 1 mouse were available anyway.\n- **Result**: 88 candidate sessions from 37 mice; 87 kept after dropping one session with no eye tracking data (experiment 806456687).\n\n### 7. Discretization: Initially Global, then Switched to Per-Session\n\nThe agent initially implemented **global percentile bins** (pooling all sessions to compute quintile edges). However, it discovered that absolute pupil area varies ~3x across sessions/mice (due to camera pixel differences), causing within-session bins to be highly imbalanced (e.g., 81% of samples in one bin). Since the decoder uses per-session projections, the agent switched to **per-session percentile bins** for both running speed and pupil diameter. This ensures equal occupancy within each session -- a better match for the decoder architecture.\n\n### 8. Quality Controls\n\n- **Neural data**: No additional neuron filtering beyond what the Allen SDK already applies (ROI filtering, demixing, etc.). The agent noted that some sessions with very few cells (7-13 Sst/Vip neurons) produce trials with all-zero neural data due to event sparsity, but these were kept as genuine data.\n- **Pupil data**: Blinks removed via `likely_blink` flag. Gaps interpolated. Frames more than 0.5s from a valid measurement flagged as invalid. Trials with no valid pupil data excluded. One entire session dropped for having zero eye-tracking rows.\n- **Sessions**: Required at least 2 trials per session for decoder evaluation.\n\n### 9. Missing Data Handling\n\n- **Missing pupil**: Blink-gap interpolation for short gaps; frames far from valid samples flagged. Trials with no valid pupil excluded. One session entirely dropped for missing eye tracking.\n- **Missing neural**: No special handling -- sparse events producing all-zero trial matrices were kept.\n- **No decoder inputs**: The task specifies no inputs, so input arrays are shape (0, T). The agent verified the decoder accepts zero-dimensional inputs with a synthetic test.\n\n### 10. Key Reasoning Patterns\n\nThe agent was notably empirical: it ran **controlled pilot experiments** comparing events vs filtered_events, global vs per-session binning, and with vs without per-neuron normalization, using decoder accuracy as the criterion. Each decision was backed by a quantitative comparison on a subset of the data before committing to the full run. The agent also performed sanity checks (spot-checking a converted trial against raw data, confirming the dropped session genuinely lacked eye tracking, verifying trial counts). The trial window was defined by the trials table `start_time` to `stop_time` rather than a fixed window around change time, using the natural trial boundaries from the experiment."},{"type":"text","text":"agentId: a7fe70a380aaada83 (use SendMessage with to: 'a7fe70a380aaada83' to continue this agent)\n<usage>total_tokens: 35898\ntool_uses: 10\nduration_ms: 79878</usage>"}]}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"37295e86-b481-40a5-85ff-ae25f3e2d47a","uuid":"f35d74f5-383d-43ab-8cad-347c6d2fe15e","timestamp":"2026-09-23T17:23:27.189Z","tool_use_result":{"status":"completed","prompt":"Read /logs/agent/trajectory.json and extract a summary of the key decisions the AI agent made when building its convert_data.py.
